It’s more… I don’t know if I enjoy things the way I used to, or if I’m just doing them because they’re part of my routine. Does that count as losing interest?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the sadness or heaviness is interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I don’t always want to admit that, because I’m still getting up and going to work and all that, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups at the credit office. I’ll tell myself I’ll call them after lunch, then somehow it becomes tomorrow.\n\nAnd I’ve stepped back from things I used to make sure I went to, like gallery events and the walking club. I used to like seeing what local artists were doing—there was one woman who painted these little street scenes, I always liked those—but lately I just don’t make myself go. So… yes, it’s getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this dull heaviness or sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. Since my close friend died.\n\nI remember because it was around the time I was trying to get some paperwork sorted at the office, and everything felt like it just froze. Funny what you remember—stacks of forms, and I kept thinking I should bring her those lemon cookies she liked, and then… well, I couldn’t.\n\nSo yes, the heaviness has been there since then. Over two years.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of at least two weeks where you felt better and the sadness or heaviness lifted?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are days where I’m busier, like if I go to the community center or I’m out in the garden trying to keep the squirrels from ruining everything—honestly, they’re relentless—but even then it’s not like the heaviness is gone.\n\nMost days it’s just sort of sitting there with me. I can smile and talk to people, and I probably look fine enough, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I can’t think of a stretch of two weeks where it really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer take pleasure in activities that you once found enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “no longer,” exactly. I mean… I still sometimes like things once I’m there, if that makes sense. Like a gallery opening, I might look at a painting and think, oh, that’s nice, or I’ll notice the color of someone’s scarf more than the art, honestly.\n\nBut getting myself to go is a whole other matter. And the walking club—well, they changed the route a while back, and I didn’t care for that hill near the church, it’s harder on my knees than people admit. So I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I just don’t seem to reach for them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, after she died I thought about death a lot, but more like… how strange it is that someone can be here, and then their coffee mug is still in the cabinet, you know?\n\nI remember helping sort a few things, and there was this blue sweater she had—terrible color on me, but she wore it beautifully—and I kept thinking, what are we supposed to do with all these objects people leave behind?\n\nSo… I guess I’ve thought about death in that way. But if you mean something more specific, I’m not sure I’m saying it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, my clothes mostly fit the same, though some days I blame the dryer for everything shrinking—my sister does that too, she swears it’s the dryer and not the cookies.\n\nBut no, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or anything. I can’t point to a big change since she passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I don’t know… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something after someone dies. You think, oh, I should’ve called more, or I shouldn’t have let that last lunch be so rushed.\n\nBut since then? I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes I’ll remember little things, like she always returned library books late, and I’d tease her about it—there was this one librarian who knew us by name, actually, very stern woman, wore these little glasses on a chain. I don’t know why I remember that.\n\nSo… I guess I have moments where I wonder if I did enough, but I don’t know that I’d say I often feel guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that used to give you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still go to the community center sometimes, and I’ll putter around in my garden—well, when the rabbits haven’t treated it like an all-you-can-eat buffet, which they do, the little thieves.\n\nThose things used to really lift me up. I’d come home from the center feeling lighter, or I’d lose track of time with the plants. Lately, most days, it’s more like there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me. I can be there, doing the same things, even smiling at people, but it feels kind of pasted on. Like I’m going through the motions more than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you once found enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not that I don’t do anything anymore—I still go to the community center sometimes, and I still fuss with the garden. The tomatoes did terribly this year, by the way, though my neighbor says everyone’s did, so maybe it wasn’t just me.\n\nBut those things used to actually lift me up. Now most days there’s this dull heaviness with me, even when I’m there. I can smile and chat, or pull weeds, or whatever, but it feels more like I’m just going through the motions… like the smile is pasted on rather than something I really feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness is worse than it was initially after your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s worse than right after she passed… that time was sharp, you know? Like everything reminded me of her. I remember finding one of those little notes she used to tuck into books—well, not mine, library books, which always seemed risky to me—and I just fell apart.\n\nNow it’s different. Most days it’s more of this dull heaviness sitting with me. Even when I’m at the community center or out in the garden, places that used to make me feel lighter, I’m just sort of… there. I can smile and make conversation, but it feels pasted on, like I’m going through the motions. So maybe not worse exactly, but it’s stayed with me more than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness interferes with your daily life almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think it does. I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s harder to keep up with things.\n\nAt the credit office I’ve started putting off client follow-ups, which isn’t really like me. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch becomes tomorrow. And then I feel bad about it, because people are counting on me—money worries don’t exactly wait politely in the hallway, you know.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been stepping back from things too. The gallery events, the walking club outings… I used to make a point of going, even if the weather was ridiculous or someone picked a route with too many hills. Lately I just… don’t. I tell myself I’m tired or that I’ll go next time, but next time keeps slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I mean, I can still show up to things. I’ll go to the community center, or I’ll be out in the garden pretending I know what I’m doing with the soil—my neighbor has very strong opinions about compost, by the way.\n\nBut those places used to really lift me up. Now most days there’s just this dull heaviness sitting with me, even there. I might smile or chat with people, but it feels sort of pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions rather than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but then I think, well, laundry does strange things too—my dryer has opinions, I swear. And I haven’t stepped on a scale in a while. So I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something after someone dies. You think, oh, I should’ve called more, or I shouldn’t have let that last lunch be so rushed.\n\nBut since then? I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes I’ll remember little things, like she always returned library books late, and I’d tease her about it—there was this one librarian who knew us by name, actually, very stern woman, wore these little glasses on a chain. I don’t know why I remember that.\n\nSo… I guess I have moments where I wonder if I did enough, but I don’t know that I’d say I often feel guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that used to give you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still go to the community center sometimes, and I’ll putter around in my garden—well, when the rabbits haven’t treated it like an all-you-can-eat buffet, which they do, the little thieves.\n\nThose things used to really lift me up. I’d come home from the center feeling lighter, or I’d lose track of time with the plants. Lately, most days, it’s more like there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me. I can be there, doing the same things, even smiling at people, but it feels kind of pasted on. Like I’m going through the motions more than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you once found enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not that I don’t do anything anymore—I still go to the community center sometimes, and I still fuss with the garden. The tomatoes did terribly this year, by the way, though my neighbor says everyone’s did, so maybe it wasn’t just me.\n\nBut those things used to actually lift me up. Now most days there’s this dull heaviness with me, even when I’m there. I can smile and chat, or pull weeds, or whatever, but it feels more like I’m just going through the motions… like the smile is pasted on rather than something I really feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness is worse than it was initially after your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s worse than right after she passed… that time was sharp, you know? Like everything reminded me of her. I remember finding one of those little notes she used to tuck into books—well, not mine, library books, which always seemed risky to me—and I just fell apart.\n\nNow it’s different. Most days it’s more of this dull heaviness sitting with me. Even when I’m at the community center or out in the garden, places that used to make me feel lighter, I’m just sort of… there. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch becomes tomorrow. And then I feel bad about it, because people are counting on me—money worries don’t exactly wait politely in the hallway, you know.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been stepping back from things too. The gallery events, the walking club outings… I used to make a point of going, even if the weather was ridiculous or someone picked a route with too many hills. Lately I just… don’t. I tell myself I’m tired or that I’ll go next time, but next time keeps slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I mean, I can still show up to things. I’ll go to the community center, or I’ll be out in the garden pretending I know what I’m doing with the soil—my neighbor has very strong opinions about compost, by the way.\n\nBut those places used to really lift me up. Now most days there’s just this dull heaviness sitting with me, even there. I might smile or chat with people, but it feels sort of pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions rather than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. Yes… it’s been a little over two years now, since my close friend died. I keep thinking of it by seasons, you know—like, “that was the first winter after,” and then somehow another one came and went. Funny how calendars just keep flipping even when you feel sort of stuck.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because my mind just isn’t working the way it used to. At work, I’ll be looking at a loan worksheet or a client follow-up list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still don’t know what it says. Then I put off making calls because even simple decisions feel… bigger than they should.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve done this kind of work for years, and I used to be pretty steady with it. I mean, I still keep my desk organized—well, mostly, except my grandson gave me this little calendar thing and it keeps falling behind the monitor—but anyway, I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things lately.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice your mind wasn't working the way it used to?\n\nPatient: It was… a little over two years ago, I think. Around when my close friend died. I remember because I kept thinking I was just distracted from all of that, you know, grief makes everything feel sort of foggy. She and I used to talk during lunch sometimes, even about silly things like what was on sale at the grocery store, and after she was gone, I’d catch myself just staring at papers at work. So… yes, that’s when I first really noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. It’s not always crying or anything dramatic, it’s more like this dull heaviness that just sits with me.\n\nI still go to the community center sometimes, and I’ll be in my garden too—my tomatoes did better this year than I expected, actually—but even in those places that used to make me feel lighter, I feel like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a smile pasted on, but inside I’m not really there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I still do some of the same things—I go to the community center now and then, and I fuss with my garden, though the squirrels have been terrible this year, just digging like they own the place.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped everything. It’s more… I don’t know if I enjoy things the way I used to, or if I’m just doing them because they’re part of my routine. Does that count as losing interest?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the sadness or heaviness is interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I don’t always want to admit that, because I’m still getting up and going to work and all that, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups at the credit office. I’ll tell myself I’ll call them after lunch, then somehow it becomes tomorrow.\n\nAnd I’ve stepped back from things I used to make sure I went to, like gallery events and the walking club. I used to like seeing what local artists were doing—there was one woman who painted these little street scenes, I always liked those—but lately I just don’t make myself go. So… yes, it’s getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this dull heaviness or sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. Since my close friend died.\n\nI remember because it was around the time I was trying to get some paperwork sorted at the office, and everything felt like it just froze. Funny what you remember—stacks of forms, and I kept thinking I should bring her those lemon cookies she liked, and then… well, I couldn’t.\n\nSo yes, the heaviness has been there since then. Over two years.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of at least two weeks where you felt better and the sadness or heaviness lifted?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are days where I’m busier, like if I go to the community center or I’m out in the garden trying to keep the squirrels from ruining everything—honestly, they’re relentless—but even then it’s not like the heaviness is gone.\n\nMost days it’s just sort of sitting there with me. I can smile and talk to people, and I probably look fine enough, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I can’t think of a stretch of two weeks where it really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer take pleasure in activities that you once found enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “no longer,” exactly. I mean… I still sometimes like things once I’m there, if that makes sense. Like a gallery opening, I might look at a painting and think, oh, that’s nice, or I’ll notice the color of someone’s scarf more than the art, honestly.\n\nBut getting myself to go is a whole other matter. And the walking club—well, they changed the route a while back, and I didn’t care for that hill near the church, it’s harder on my knees than people admit. So I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I just don’t seem to reach for them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, after she died I thought about death a lot, but more like… how strange it is that someone can be here, and then their coffee mug is still in the cabinet, you know?\n\nI remember helping sort a few things, and there was this blue sweater she had—terrible color on me, but she wore it beautifully—and I kept thinking, what are we supposed to do with all these objects people leave behind?\n\nSo… I guess I’ve thought about death in that way. But if you mean something more specific, I’m not sure I’m saying it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, my clothes mostly fit the same, though some days I blame the dryer for everything shrinking—my sister does that too, she swears it’s the dryer and not the cookies.\n\nBut no, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or anything. I can’t point to a big change since she passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I don’t know… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something after someone dies. You think, oh, I should’ve called more, or I shouldn’t have let that last lunch be so rushed.\n\nBut since then? I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes I’ll remember little things, like she always returned library books late, and I’d tease her about it—there was this one librarian who knew us by name, actually, very stern woman, wore these little glasses on a chain. I don’t know why I remember that.\n\nSo… I guess I have moments where I wonder if I did enough, but I don’t know that I’d say I often feel guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that used to give you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still go to the community center sometimes, and I’ll putter around in my garden—well, when the rabbits haven’t treated it like an all-you-can-eat buffet, which they do, the little thieves.\n\nThose things used to really lift me up. I’d come home from the center feeling lighter, or I’d lose track of time with the plants. Lately, most days, it’s more like there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me. I can be there, doing the same things, even smiling at people, but it feels kind of pasted on. Like I’m going through the motions more than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you once found enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not that I don’t do anything anymore—I still go to the community center sometimes, and I still fuss with the garden. The tomatoes did terribly this year, by the way, though my neighbor says everyone’s did, so maybe it wasn’t just me.\n\nBut those things used to actually lift me up. Now most days there’s this dull heaviness with me, even when I’m there. I can smile and chat, or pull weeds, or whatever, but it feels more like I’m just going through the motions… like the smile is pasted on rather than something I really feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness is worse than it was initially after your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s worse than right after she passed… that time was sharp, you know? Like everything reminded me of her. I remember finding one of those little notes she used to tuck into books—well, not mine, library books, which always seemed risky to me—and I just fell apart.\n\nNow it’s different. Most days it’s more of this dull heaviness sitting with me. Even when I’m at the community center or out in the garden, places that used to make me feel lighter, I’m just sort of… there. I can smile and make conversation, but it feels pasted on, like I’m going through the motions. So maybe not worse exactly, but it’s stayed with me more than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness interferes with your daily life almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think it does. I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s harder to keep up with things.\n\nAt the credit office I’ve started putting off client follow-ups, which isn’t really like me. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch becomes tomorrow. And then I feel bad about it, because people are counting on me—money worries don’t exactly wait politely in the hallway, you know.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been stepping back from things too. The gallery events, the walking club outings… I used to make a point of going, even if the weather was ridiculous or someone picked a route with too many hills. Lately I just… don’t. I tell myself I’m tired or that I’ll go next time, but next time keeps slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I mean, I can still show up to things. I’ll go to the community center, or I’ll be out in the garden pretending I know what I’m doing with the soil—my neighbor has very strong opinions about compost, by the way.\n\nBut those places used to really lift me up. Now most days there’s just this dull heaviness sitting with me, even there. I might smile or chat with people, but it feels sort of pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions rather than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but then I think, well, laundry does strange things too—my dryer has opinions, I swear. And I haven’t stepped on a scale in a while. So I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to find pleasure in things that you used to enjoy, most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say. I still do the things, or at least I try to. I’ll go over to the community center, or I’ll putter around in the garden—though half the time I end up standing there wondering if I watered the same plant twice and forgot the others.\n\nBut it’s not like it used to be. Those things used to lift me up a little. Now there’s this dull heaviness that sort of comes along with me. I can smile and make conversation, but it feels pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions more than really enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Has your sadness or heaviness lasted for more than two years without any breaks?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been a little over two years now, since my close friend died. I keep thinking of it by seasons, you know—like, “that was the first winter after,” and then somehow another one came and went. Funny how calendars just keep flipping even when you feel sort of stuck.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s lasted more than two years. I can’t really point to a real break from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

I can smile and make conversation, but it feels pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions more than really enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Has your sadness or heaviness lasted for more than two years without any breaks?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been a little over two years now, since my close friend died. I keep thinking of it by seasons, you know—like, “that was the first winter after,” and then somehow another one came and went. Funny how calendars just keep flipping even when you feel sort of stuck.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s lasted more than two years. I can’t really point to a real break from it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any periods where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least two months since your friend passed away?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F32.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because my mind just isn’t working the way it used to. At work, I’ll be looking at a loan worksheet or a client follow-up list, and I’ll realize I’ve read the same line three or four times and still don’t know what it says. Then I put off making calls because even simple decisions feel… bigger than they should.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I’ve done this kind of work for years, and I used to be pretty steady with it. I mean, I still keep my desk organized—well, mostly, except my grandson gave me this little calendar thing and it keeps falling behind the monitor—but anyway, I just can’t seem to keep my mind on things lately.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice your mind wasn't working the way it used to?\n\nPatient: It was… a little over two years ago, I think. Around when my close friend died. I remember because I kept thinking I was just distracted from all of that, you know, grief makes everything feel sort of foggy. She and I used to talk during lunch sometimes, even about silly things like what was on sale at the grocery store, and after she was gone, I’d catch myself just staring at papers at work. So… yes, that’s when I first really noticed it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days, I’d say. It’s not always crying or anything dramatic, it’s more like this dull heaviness that just sits with me.\n\nI still go to the community center sometimes, and I’ll be in my garden too—my tomatoes did better this year than I expected, actually—but even in those places that used to make me feel lighter, I feel like I’m just going through the motions. Like I’ve got a smile pasted on, but inside I’m not really there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any loss of interest in activities you once enjoyed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I still do some of the same things—I go to the community center now and then, and I fuss with my garden, though the squirrels have been terrible this year, just digging like they own the place.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped everything. It’s more… I don’t know if I enjoy things the way I used to, or if I’m just doing them because they’re part of my routine. Does that count as losing interest?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel the sadness or heaviness is interfering with your daily life most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I don’t always want to admit that, because I’m still getting up and going to work and all that, but I’ve started putting off client follow-ups at the credit office. I’ll tell myself I’ll call them after lunch, then somehow it becomes tomorrow.\n\nAnd I’ve stepped back from things I used to make sure I went to, like gallery events and the walking club. I used to like seeing what local artists were doing—there was one woman who painted these little street scenes, I always liked those—but lately I just don’t make myself go. So… yes, it’s getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this dull heaviness or sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years now, I think. Since my close friend died.\n\nI remember because it was around the time I was trying to get some paperwork sorted at the office, and everything felt like it just froze. Funny what you remember—stacks of forms, and I kept thinking I should bring her those lemon cookies she liked, and then… well, I couldn’t.\n\nSo yes, the heaviness has been there since then. Over two years.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of at least two weeks where you felt better and the sadness or heaviness lifted?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, there are days where I’m busier, like if I go to the community center or I’m out in the garden trying to keep the squirrels from ruining everything—honestly, they’re relentless—but even then it’s not like the heaviness is gone.\n\nMost days it’s just sort of sitting there with me. I can smile and talk to people, and I probably look fine enough, but inside it feels like I’m just going through the motions. I can’t think of a stretch of two weeks where it really lifted.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer take pleasure in activities that you once found enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “no longer,” exactly. I mean… I still sometimes like things once I’m there, if that makes sense. Like a gallery opening, I might look at a painting and think, oh, that’s nice, or I’ll notice the color of someone’s scarf more than the art, honestly.\n\nBut getting myself to go is a whole other matter. And the walking club—well, they changed the route a while back, and I didn’t care for that hill near the church, it’s harder on my knees than people admit. So I don’t know if it’s that I don’t enjoy things anymore, or if I just don’t seem to reach for them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, after she died I thought about death a lot, but more like… how strange it is that someone can be here, and then their coffee mug is still in the cabinet, you know?\n\nI remember helping sort a few things, and there was this blue sweater she had—terrible color on me, but she wore it beautifully—and I kept thinking, what are we supposed to do with all these objects people leave behind?\n\nSo… I guess I’ve thought about death in that way. But if you mean something more specific, I’m not sure I’m saying it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I’ve really noticed. I mean, my clothes mostly fit the same, though some days I blame the dryer for everything shrinking—my sister does that too, she swears it’s the dryer and not the cookies.\n\nBut no, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly or anything. I can’t point to a big change since she passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that have happened since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Guilty? I don’t know… I mean, I suppose everyone feels guilty about something after someone dies. You think, oh, I should’ve called more, or I shouldn’t have let that last lunch be so rushed.\n\nBut since then? I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes I’ll remember little things, like she always returned library books late, and I’d tease her about it—there was this one librarian who knew us by name, actually, very stern woman, wore these little glasses on a chain. I don’t know why I remember that.\n\nSo… I guess I have moments where I wonder if I did enough, but I don’t know that I’d say I often feel guilty.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer enjoy things that used to give you pleasure?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I still go to the community center sometimes, and I’ll putter around in my garden—well, when the rabbits haven’t treated it like an all-you-can-eat buffet, which they do, the little thieves.\n\nThose things used to really lift me up. I’d come home from the center feeling lighter, or I’d lose track of time with the plants. Lately, most days, it’s more like there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me. I can be there, doing the same things, even smiling at people, but it feels kind of pasted on. Like I’m going through the motions more than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you once found enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not that I don’t do anything anymore—I still go to the community center sometimes, and I still fuss with the garden. The tomatoes did terribly this year, by the way, though my neighbor says everyone’s did, so maybe it wasn’t just me.\n\nBut those things used to actually lift me up. Now most days there’s this dull heaviness with me, even when I’m there. I can smile and chat, or pull weeds, or whatever, but it feels more like I’m just going through the motions… like the smile is pasted on rather than something I really feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness is worse than it was initially after your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s worse than right after she passed… that time was sharp, you know? Like everything reminded me of her. I remember finding one of those little notes she used to tuck into books—well, not mine, library books, which always seemed risky to me—and I just fell apart.\n\nNow it’s different. Most days it’s more of this dull heaviness sitting with me. Even when I’m at the community center or out in the garden, places that used to make me feel lighter, I’m just sort of… there. I can smile and make conversation, but it feels pasted on, like I’m going through the motions. So maybe not worse exactly, but it’s stayed with me more than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness interferes with your daily life almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think it does. I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s harder to keep up with things.\n\nAt the credit office I’ve started putting off client follow-ups, which isn’t really like me. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch becomes tomorrow. And then I feel bad about it, because people are counting on me—money worries don’t exactly wait politely in the hallway, you know.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been stepping back from things too. The gallery events, the walking club outings… I used to make a point of going, even if the weather was ridiculous or someone picked a route with too many hills. Lately I just… don’t. I tell myself I’m tired or that I’ll go next time, but next time keeps slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I mean, I can still show up to things. I’ll go to the community center, or I’ll be out in the garden pretending I know what I’m doing with the soil—my neighbor has very strong opinions about compost, by the way.\n\nBut those places used to really lift me up. Now most days there’s just this dull heaviness sitting with me, even there. I might smile or chat with people, but it feels sort of pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions rather than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but then I think, well, laundry does strange things too—my dryer has opinions, I swear. And I haven’t stepped on a scale in a while. So I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to find pleasure in things that you used to enjoy, most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say. I still do the things, or at least I try to. I’ll go over to the community center, or I’ll putter around in the garden—though half the time I end up standing there wondering if I watered the same plant twice and forgot the others.\n\nBut it’s not like it used to be. Those things used to lift me up a little. Now there’s this dull heaviness that sort of comes along with me. I can smile and make conversation, but it feels pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions more than really enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Has your sadness or heaviness lasted for more than two years without any breaks?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been a little over two years now, since my close friend died. I can’t really point to a real break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least two months since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had a stretch like that. No… I can’t say that’s happened.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I still go to the community center sometimes, and I’ll putter around in my garden—well, when the rabbits haven’t treated it like an all-you-can-eat buffet, which they do, the little thieves.\n\nThose things used to really lift me up. I’d come home from the center feeling lighter, or I’d lose track of time with the plants. Lately, most days, it’s more like there’s this dull heaviness sitting with me. I can be there, doing the same things, even smiling at people, but it feels kind of pasted on. Like I’m going through the motions more than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you once found enjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not that I don’t do anything anymore—I still go to the community center sometimes, and I still fuss with the garden. The tomatoes did terribly this year, by the way, though my neighbor says everyone’s did, so maybe it wasn’t just me.\n\nBut those things used to actually lift me up. Now most days there’s this dull heaviness with me, even when I’m there. I can smile and chat, or pull weeds, or whatever, but it feels more like I’m just going through the motions… like the smile is pasted on rather than something I really feel.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness is worse than it was initially after your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s worse than right after she passed… that time was sharp, you know? Like everything reminded me of her. I remember finding one of those little notes she used to tuck into books—well, not mine, library books, which always seemed risky to me—and I just fell apart.\n\nNow it’s different. Most days it’s more of this dull heaviness sitting with me. Even when I’m at the community center or out in the garden, places that used to make me feel lighter, I’m just sort of… there. I can smile and make conversation, but it feels pasted on, like I’m going through the motions. So maybe not worse exactly, but it’s stayed with me more than I expected.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your sadness or heaviness interferes with your daily life almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think it does. I still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it’s harder to keep up with things.\n\nAt the credit office I’ve started putting off client follow-ups, which isn’t really like me. I’ll tell myself, “I’ll call them after lunch,” and then after lunch becomes tomorrow. And then I feel bad about it, because people are counting on me—money worries don’t exactly wait politely in the hallway, you know.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve been stepping back from things too. The gallery events, the walking club outings… I used to make a point of going, even if the weather was ridiculous or someone picked a route with too many hills. Lately I just… don’t. I tell myself I’m tired or that I’ll go next time, but next time keeps slipping away.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. I mean, I can still show up to things. I’ll go to the community center, or I’ll be out in the garden pretending I know what I’m doing with the soil—my neighbor has very strong opinions about compost, by the way.\n\nBut those places used to really lift me up. Now most days there’s just this dull heaviness sitting with me, even there. I might smile or chat with people, but it feels sort of pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions rather than actually enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I haven’t really been tracking it.\n\nMy clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but then I think, well, laundry does strange things too—my dryer has opinions, I swear. And I haven’t stepped on a scale in a while. So I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to find pleasure in things that you used to enjoy, most of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I’d say. I still do the things, or at least I try to. I’ll go over to the community center, or I’ll putter around in the garden—though half the time I end up standing there wondering if I watered the same plant twice and forgot the others.\n\nBut it’s not like it used to be. Those things used to lift me up a little. Now there’s this dull heaviness that sort of comes along with me. I can smile and make conversation, but it feels pasted on, like I’m just going through the motions more than really enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Has your sadness or heaviness lasted for more than two years without any breaks?\n\nPatient: Yes… it’s been a little over two years now, since my close friend died. I keep thinking of it by seasons, you know—like, “that was the first winter after,” and then somehow another one came and went. Funny how calendars just keep flipping even when you feel sort of stuck.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s lasted more than two years. I can’t really point to a real break from it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where you felt completely back to your normal self for at least two months since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had a stretch like that. It’s been going on since my close friend died, a little over two years ago.\n\nI mean, there are days where I’m distracted enough—work gets busy, or I’m sorting through paperwork and suddenly three hours have gone by. Funny thing, paperwork can swallow a whole afternoon if you let it. But feeling completely like my old self for two months? No… I can’t say that’s happened."
